10.7 · The Killing of the Demon Tṛṇāvarta

    A Attacking Asuras And Astonishing Activities SB 10.6–10.14 Read summary →

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.1-2

    ↗

    Important Words
    • karṇa-ramyāṇi — pleasing to the ear
      Compound: karṇa (ear) + ramyāṇi (delightful). The Bhāgavatam locates the entry-point of bhakti at the ear, not the intellect. Caitanya Mahāprabhu's prescription for Kali-yuga — śravaṇaṁ kīrtanaṁ — rests on this verse's claim.
    • vitṛṣṇā — the dirty things within the mind
      Literally 'absence of thirst.' Prabhupāda glosses it as the inner dirt that keeps one uninterested in Kṛṣṇa. Hearing kṛṣṇa-kathā does not merely add devotion; it removes the obstruction to it — ceto-darpaṇa-mārjanam.
    • bhaktiḥ harau — devotional attachment to the Lord
      Locative with Harau — bhakti is directed at a Person, not at an abstraction. The Gauḍīya frame insists bhakti always has a viṣaya (object); generic 'love' or 'devotion' without Hari is not bhakti.
    • tat-puruṣe — with the Lord's devotee (Vaiṣṇava)
      Tat (His) + puruṣe (person). The verse equates attraction to the Lord's people with bhakti itself — the Gauḍīya doctrine that sādhu-saṅga and Hari-bhakti are inseparable, both arising from the same śravaṇa.
    • sakhyam — attraction to association
      Friendship, fellowship. Prabhupāda often quoted Caitanya's words 'sādhu-saṅga, sādhu-saṅga sarva-śāstre kaya' — without devotee-association, no advancement. Here sakhyam is the third fruit of śravaṇa, after purification and bhakti.
    • hāram — garland (of pastimes worn on the neck)
      From hṛ — that which is taken and worn. Prabhupāda's gloss: kṛṣṇa-kathā is to be worn on the neck like a hāra, kept close, not stored on a shelf. The metaphor reframes hearing as ornament-wearing — a devotional act, not information intake.
    King Parīkṣit said: My lord, Śukadeva Gosvāmī, all the various activities exhibited by the incarnations of the Supreme Personality of Godhead are certainly pleasing to the ear and to the mind. Simply by one’s hearing of these activities, the dirty things in one’s mind immediately vanish. Generally we are reluctant to hear about the activities of the Lord, but Kṛṣṇa’s childhood activities are so attractive that they are automatically pleasing to the mind and ear. Thus one’s attachment for hearing about material things, which is the root cause of material existence, vanishes, and one gradually develops devotional service to the Supreme Lord, attachment for Him, and friendship with devotees who give us the contribution of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. If you think it fit, kindly speak about those activities of the Lord.
    Purport

    As stated in the Prema-vivarta: kṛṣṇa-bahirmukha haiyā bhoga-vāñchā kare nikaṭa-stha māyā tāre jāpaṭiyā dhare Our material existence is māyā, or illusion, in which we desire different varieties of material enjoyment and therefore change to different varieties of bodies ( bhrāmayan sarva-bhūtāni yantrārūḍhāni māyayā ). Asann api kleśada āsa dehaḥ: as long as we have these temporary bodies, they give us many varieties of tribulation — ādhyātmika, ādhibhautika and ādhidaivika. This is the root cause of all suffering, but this root cause of suffering can be removed by revival of our Kṛṣṇa consciousness. All the Vedic literatures presented by Vyāsadeva and other great sages are therefore intended to revive our Kṛṣṇa consciousness, which begins to revive with śravaṇa-kīrtanam: śṛṇvatāṁ sva-kathāḥ kṛṣṇaḥ ( Bhāg. 1.2.17 ). Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam and other Vedic literatures exist simply to give us a chance to hear about Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa has different avatāras, or incarnations, all of which are wonderful and which arouse one’s inquisitiveness, but generally such avatāras as Matsya, Kūrma and Varāha are not as attractive as Kṛṣṇa. First of all, however, we have no attraction for hearing about Kṛṣṇa, and this is the root cause of our suffering. But Parīkṣit Mahārāja specifically mentions that the wonderful activities of baby Kṛṣṇa, which amazed mother Yaśodā and the other inhabitants of Vraja, are especially attractive. From the very beginning of His childhood, Kṛṣṇa killed Pūtanā, Tṛṇāvarta and Śakaṭāsura and showed the entire universe within His mouth. Thus the pastimes of Kṛṣṇa, one after another, kept mother Yaśodā and all the inhabitants of Vraja in great astonishment. The process to revive one’s Kṛṣṇa consciousness is ādau śraddhā tataḥ sādhu-saṅgaḥ ( Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu 1.4.15). The pastimes of Kṛṣṇa can be properly received from devotees. If one has developed a little bit of Kṛṣṇa consciousness by hearing from Vaiṣṇavas about the activities of Kṛṣṇa, one becomes attached to Vaiṣṇavas who are interested only in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Therefore Parīkṣit Mahārāja recommends that one hear about Kṛṣṇa’s childhood activities, which are more attractive than the activities of other incarnations, such as Matsya, Kūrma and Varāha. Wanting to hear more and more from Śukadeva Gosvāmī, Mahārāja Parīkṣit requested him to continue describing Kṛṣṇa’s childhood activities, which are especially easy to hear and which create more and more inquisitiveness.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    King Parīkṣit said: My lord, Śukadeva Gosvāmī, all the various activities exhibited by the incarnations of the Supreme Personality of Godhead are certainly pleasing to the ear and to the mind. Simply by one's hearing of these activities, the dirty things in one's mind immediately vanish. Generally we are reluctant to hear about the activities of the Lord, but Kṛṣṇa's childhood activities are so attractive that they are automatically pleasing to the mind and ear. Thus one's attachment for hearing about material things, which is the root cause of material existence, vanishes, and one gradually develops devotional service to the Supreme Lord, attachment for Him, and friendship with devotees who give us the contribution of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. If you think it fit, kindly speak about those activities of the Lord.
    Commentary

    The seventh chapter shows the aisvarya (display of power) aspect of Krsna’s childhood pastimes. After bathing and sleeping he broke the cart. He killed Trnavarta and showed his mother the universe. By chewing Putana’s breast he showed the power of his lips. In the cart pastime he showed the power of his feet, and killing Trnavarta he showed the power of his hands. In revealing the universe twice (in this and the next chapter) to his mother he showed all his powers. "My mind is attracted to all the pastimes of the Lord’s other avataras, but I am particularly greedy to hear Krsna’s childhood pastimes." With this intent, Pariksit speaks the verse. "I relish hearing all the pastimes of the avataras of the Lord and I become blissful. But, if you agree, please speak about those pastimes (like a necklace over the heart-haram) which destroy any human being’s apathy and distaste upon hearing them, which bring out a hankering for them, and which purify the mind of bad desires, like rock candy which first tastes bitter but then gives the ability to taste its sweetness, which quickly produce attachment (asakti), rati (bhava) and prema (bhakti), and brings about, even from the beginning, friendship with the devotees, and in prema causeless friendship with the devotees." Though hearing any the topics of the Lord gradually rids one of indifference to hearing about the Lord and makes it possible to attain prema, hearing the pastimes of Krsna’s childhood quickly bring about this prema.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The previous chapter stated that hearing the childhood pastimes will produce rati. Hearing of this goal, the highest result for devotees continually practicing hearing and chanting, Parīkṣit became filled with bliss, realizing that the topics were a stimulus for himself. With great eagerness, fearing a change to other topics, in two verses he welcomes narrations of the unlimited pastimes of Kṛṣṇa in order to request Śukadeva to continue with more stories about Kṛṣṇa. Bhagavān indicates the Lord endowed with all powers and qualities. Hari indicates the Lord who takes away all suffering and faults. Īśvara indicates the Lord who attracts the internal and external senses. Thus a person must serve him, otherwise he should be punished. The words karṇa-ramyāṇi and manojṣānī indicate the sweetness of the Lord. O master (prabho), endowed all powers, you know all the conditions of our senses! Bhakti or prema to the Lord, who attracts (harau) the people of Vraja and me, appears and friendship with the devotees (tat-puruṣe) also appears. He makes the request. The mind has no inclination to hearing about you, and even if it has, there are various other desires. But hearing about Kṛṣṇa vanquishes both disinclination and material desires. The heart (sattvam) becomes purified. When the heart becomes pure because of destruction of impure impressions, it becomes capable of comprehending rasa. This purification happens quickly by hearing all the pastimes of Kṛṣṇa, but more slowly in the case of hearing the pastimes of the avatāras. Though I have little intelligence, this is what I think. This will become a conviction if you, who have the greatest intelligence, agree. This is the meaning of “If you think like this, then tell these stories.” This statement is made with great humility. Or the meaning can be “Now, do not hide these topics.” This is a statement of humility mixed with joking.

    Personal Guidance
    Hearing baby Kṛṣṇa's pastimes from devotees melts the mind's dirt, cuts material craving, and naturally awakens bhakti and sādhu-saṅga.
    Practical Applications
    • Begin today by hearing or reading one pastime of baby Kṛṣṇa from a Vaiṣṇava source — aloud, slowly, with attention to the ear and heart, not as information.
    • Notice one craving (food, scrolling, validation) that flares up today and, instead of indulging or suppressing it, replace that exact moment with five minutes of śravaṇa about Kṛṣṇa.
    • Reach out to one devotee whose company increases your taste for Kṛṣṇa-kathā, and arrange a real conversation with them this week — guard sādhu-saṅga as concretely as you guard your calendar.
    Analogy

    Like a child holding a smudged copper lamp under running water — the dirt loosens by no effort of the child but by the contact of the stream. Kṛṣṇa's bāla-līlā, heard from a Vaiṣṇava's mouth, is that stream; the mind's vitṛṣṇā falls away as a side-effect of the contact, not by any technique you apply.

    Stories
    Parīkṣit at the bank of the Gaṅgā

    Cursed to die in seven days, Mahārāja Parīkṣit sat at Śuka's feet and asked only one thing — to hear Kṛṣṇa-kathā. He neither protested his fate nor sought a counter-curse; he simply opened his ear and let the stream flow.

    The Bhāgavatam itself is the fruit of that one act of śravaṇa. Parīkṣit's question here in 10.7.1 — kindly speak about those activities of the Lord — is the question of a dying man who has discovered that hearing Kṛṣṇa is enough.

    The four Kumāras and the lotus feet

    Brahmā's sons, the Kumāras, were impersonalists from birth, content to rove the universe absorbed in Brahman. But when they wandered into Vaikuṇṭha and the breeze from Viṣṇu's lotus feet, carrying the scent of tulasī, touched their nostrils, their minds were stolen (SB 3.15.43).

    They had not tried to become devotees. Contact with the Lord's feature did the work. Parīkṣit's verse names the same mechanism: yat-śṛṇvataḥ — of one who simply hears, the dirty things vanish.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.3

    Important Words
    • kṛṣṇasya — of child Kṛṣṇa
      Possessive of Kṛṣṇa — the name itself means 'all-attractive' (from kṛṣ, to attract). The Bhāgavatam (1.3.28) declares kṛṣṇas tu bhagavān svayam — Kṛṣṇa is svayaṁ-bhagavān, the source from whom all other avatāras emerge, not a derivative.
    • toka-ācaritam adbhutam — wonderful childhood pastimes
      Toka (child) + ācarita (conduct) + adbhuta (wonderful). The Vraja-līlās are simultaneously human-seeming and impossible — the adbhuta-rasa runs underneath every bāla-līlā, marking it as transcendental, not merely cute.
    • mānuṣam — as if playing as a human child
      'As if' is key. Kṛṣṇa's mānuṣa-līlā is not a real assumption of human nature; it is His sweetness (mādhurya) appearing in human shape. The Gītā's avajānanti māṁ mūḍhāḥ (9.11) warns against mistaking the mānuṣa appearance for limitation.
    • anurundhataḥ — who was imitating
      From anu-rudh, to follow closely, to imitate. Kṛṣṇa follows the human form — the verb is active. He chooses the imitation; it does not constrain Him. This is the Gauḍīya frame against any view that the avatāra is bound by the role.
    Please describe other pastimes of Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality, who appeared on this planet earth, imitating a human child and performing wonderful activities like killing Pūtanā.
    Purport

    Mahārāja Parīkṣit requested Śukadeva Gosvāmī to narrate other childhood pastimes exhibited by Kṛṣṇa while playing as a human child. The Supreme Personality of Godhead incarnates at different times in different planets and universes, and according to the nature of those places, He exhibits His unlimited potency. That a child sitting on the lap of his mother was able to kill the gigantic Pūtanā is extremely wonderful for the inhabitants of this planet, but on other planets the inhabitants are more advanced, and therefore the pastimes the Lord performs there are still more wonderful. Kṛṣṇa’s appearance on this planet like a human being makes us more fortunate than the demigods in the higher planets, and therefore Mahārāja Parīkṣit was very much interested in hearing about Him.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Please describe other pastimes of Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality, who appeared on this planet earth, imitating a human child and performing wonderful activities like killing Pūtanā.
    Commentary

    In his enthusiasm, Pariksit repeats his request to make clear his intention. Being requested by mankind the Lord appeared on earth (rather than being requested by devas and appearing on svarga). In this way he particularly gave blessings to the humans on earth.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    “What is the form of the Lord in your mind?” Without delays or introductions, now (atha), speak of the childhood pastimes of Kṛṣṇa, who attracts the heart by his sweet pastimes. He explains that the pastimes are astonishing because they have sweetness of form, qualities and pastimes sometimes mixed with inconceivable powers. Kṛṣṇa has made these pastimes visible to give mercy to us humans. Descending to the human world, he identifies completely with his pastimes (tajjātim).

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The childhood pastimes of Krsna are also of this nature. For this reason (ataḥ), describe the childhood pastimes of Kṛṣna, who is directly bhagavān, after he killed Pūtanā. They are amazing, though they are actions of a small child, showing great power and sweetness. He appeared in the material world or with a human form, accepting or respecting birth as a cowherd (jātim), indescribable with the highest rasa. Or he respected all the cowherd people. Though he was the supreme lord, by his infant pastimes he controlled them all by spreading prema. Or he was astonishing because, even as a small child, he displayed powers of the Lord. Or please describe the childhood pastimes of Kṛṣṇa who attracts all hearts. These pastimes are most bewildering (adbhūtam).

    Related values & verses
    Related values

    Personal Guidance
    Kṛṣṇa descends as a human child so we can taste His pastimes intimately — fortune the demigods envy; today, hear them with hungry ears.
    Practical Applications
    • Set aside ten unhurried minutes today to hear or read one of Kṛṣṇa's bāla-līlās aloud, treating the hearing itself as sādhana.
    • When you next meet a small child, remember Kṛṣṇa on Yaśodā's lap killing Pūtanā, and let that memory soften your heart toward the Lord's mādhurya.
    • Refuse the dull thought that this human birth is ordinary — name one concrete way today that you are more fortunate than the demigods because Kṛṣṇa appeared here.
    Analogy

    When a king walks the marketplace dressed as a common boy, the merchants still get a glimpse of royal carriage in the way He holds the reins of a toy chariot. Kṛṣṇa imitates a human child, yet the killing of Pūtanā shows the boy is svayaṁ-bhagavān — the disguise hides only as much as He wishes.

    Stories
    Rāma the perfect prince

    Rāma too played the role of a mortal — eating, sleeping, weeping for Sītā — and yet in single moments (lifting Śiva's bow, killing Tāḍakā as a boy with Viśvāmitra) the disguise lifted and the supreme Personality showed Himself.

    The Rāmāyaṇa, like the Bhāgavatam, alternates between mānuṣam līlā and aiśvarya-prakāśa. Parīkṣit's hunger here is for both — the sweetness of the human-seeming child and the wonder of His unconcealable Godhood.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.4

    ↗
    శ్రీశుక ఉవాచ కదాచిదౌత్థానికకౌతుకాప్లవే జన్మర్క్షయోగే సమవేతయోషితామ్ । వాదిత్రగీతద్విజమన్త్రవాచకై- శ్చకార సూనోరభిషేచనం సతీ ॥ ౪ ॥

    Important Words
    • autthānika-kautuka-āplave — the utthāna bathing ceremony of joyful celebration
      Utthāna (rising up — the child's first turning) + kautuka (festivity) + āplava (bathing). The Vedic culture marks even three-month-old motor milestones as occasions for saṁskāra — not sentimentality, but recognition that every stage of a Vaiṣṇava life belongs to Bhagavān.
    • janma-ṛkṣa-yoge — conjunction with the birth-star Rohiṇī
      Janma (birth) + ṛkṣa (constellation) + yoga (conjunction). Kṛṣṇa's nakṣatra is Rohiṇī. The ceremony is timed to the cosmic moment that mirrors His original appearance — Vedic culture aligns household saṁskāras to the heavens, not to the calendar of convenience.
    • dvija-mantra-vācakaiḥ — by the chanting of Vedic hymns by qualified brāhmaṇas
      Dvija (twice-born) + mantra + vācaka (reciter). The dvija is one whose second birth is through initiation and tapas. Mantra in this ceremony is not background music — it is the channel through which Kṛṣṇa's protection enters the household.
    • abhiṣecanam — the bathing ceremony
      From abhi-ṣic, to pour upon. Abhiṣeka is a royal coronation rite — the same word used for the bathing of deities and kings. Yaśodā bathes her son with the bhāva of installing a king, though her conscious mood is only motherly. Vraja's vātsalya always carries hidden aiśvarya.
    Śukadeva Gosvāmī said: When mother Yaśodā’s baby was slanting His body to attempt to rise and turn around, this attempt was observed by a Vedic ceremony. In such a ceremony, called utthāna, which is performed when a child is due to leave the house for the first time, the child is properly bathed. Just after Kṛṣṇa turned three months old, mother Yaśodā celebrated this ceremony with other women of the neighborhood. On that day, there was a conjunction of the moon with the constellation Rohiṇī. As the brāhmaṇas joined by chanting Vedic hymns and professional musicians also took part, this great ceremony was observed by mother Yaśodā.
    Purport

    There is no question of overpopulation or of children’s being a burden for their parents in a Vedic society. Such a society is so well organized and people are so advanced in spiritual consciousness that childbirth is never regarded as a burden or a botheration. The more a child grows, the more his parents become jubilant, and the child’s attempts to turn over are also a source of jubilation. Even before the child is born, when the mother is pregnant, many recommended ritualistic ceremonies are performed. For example, when the child has been within the womb for three months and for seven months, there is a ceremony the mother observes by eating with neighboring children. This ceremony is called svāda-bhakṣaṇa. Similarly, before the birth of the child there is the garbhādhāna ceremony. In Vedic civilization, childbirth or pregnancy is never regarded as a burden; rather, it is a cause for jubilation. In contrast, people in modern civilization do not like pregnancy or childbirth, and when there is a child, they sometimes kill it. We can just consider how human society has fallen since the inauguration of Kali-yuga. Although people still claim to be civilized, at the present moment there is actually no human civilization, but only an assembly of two-legged animals.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Śukadeva Gosvāmī said: When mother Yaśodā's baby was slanting His body to attempt to rise and turn around, this attempt was observed by a Vedic ceremony. In such a ceremony, called utthāna, which is performed when a child is due to leave the house for the first time, the child is properly bathed. Just after Kṛṣṇa turned three months old, mother Yaśodā celebrated this ceremony with other women of the neighborhood. On that day, there was a conjunction of the moon with the constellation Rohiṇī. As the brāhmaṇas joined by chanting Vedic hymns and professional musicians also took part, this great ceremony was observed by mother Yaśodā
    Commentary

    Kadacit (some time) means when three months had passed after the birth of the child this incident took place, for in the second canto (2.7.27) it is mentioned that the breaking of the cart occurred when Krsna was three months old. When the child was able to turn over (autthanik), the women became overjoyed and when the moon entered his birth star (rohini), Yasoda, surrounded by the gopis, celebrated the event with bathing of the child to the accompaniment of music and songs and chanting by brahmanas

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Pleased by Parīkṣit’s words Śukadeva began describing in order Kṛṣṇa’s childhood pastimes. This event happened when he was three months old. Trai-māsikasya ca padā śakaṭo ‘pavṛttaḥ: he overturned the cart with his three- month-old foot. (SB 2.7.27) It also happened that the moon was in Rohiṇī and thus three months from his birth according to the lunar calendar (since his birth also occurred with the moon in Rohiṇī). The event was a bath on the occasion of his raising himself from the bed. Some say this refers to going outside, but that ceremony occurs during the fourth month according to the scriptures and is calculated according to the solar calendar. Yaśodā was attached to this ceremony out of affection, since Kṛṣṇa was her only son. Yaśodā (satī) was expert in all ceremonies. Since this was a ceremony predominantly for the women, Yaśodā did this rather than Nanda, who had done the birth ceremony.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Pleased by Parīkṣit’s words Śukadeva began describing in order Kṛṣṇa’s childhood pastimes. This event happened when he was three months old. Trai-māsikasya ca padā śakaṭo ‘pavṛttaḥ: he overturned the cart with his three- month-old foot. (SB 2.7.27) It also happened that the moon was in Rohiṇī and thus three months from his birth according to the lunar calendar (since his birth also occurred with the moon in Rohiṇī). The event was a bath on the occasion of his raising himself from the bed. Some say this refers to going outside, but that ceremony occurs during the fourth month according to the scriptures and is calculated according to the solar calendar. Yaśodā was attached to this ceremony out of affection, since Kṛṣṇa was her only son. Yaśodā (satī) was expert in all ceremonies. She bathed him by sprinkling water on him, as brāhmaṇas chanted mantras, after establishing a pot. Since this was a ceremony predominantly for the women, Yaśodā did this rather than Nanda, who had done the birth ceremony.

    Personal Guidance
    Welcoming a child's every milestone with Vedic ceremony reveals a culture that treats new life as Kṛṣṇa's gift, never a burden — receive your duties the same way.
    Practical Applications
    • Mark one ordinary milestone in your family today — a child's small step, a parent's recovery, a sibling's effort — with a deliberate offering or prayer to Kṛṣṇa instead of letting it pass unnoticed.
    • Refuse the modern framing that responsibilities (children, dependents, elders, sevā) are 'burdens'; name one such duty aloud today as a cause for jubilation.
    • Invite saṅga into a family moment this week — call a devotee, play kīrtana during a meal, or chant together — so that household life is sanctified by Vaiṣṇava association rather than left secular.
    Analogy

    A new lamp lit in the household is greeted with garlands, music, and brāhmaṇa-mantras — not because the lamp needs blessing but because the house has been given a flame to keep. Kṛṣṇa's first turning of His body is welcomed the same way: not for His sake, but to teach the household to honor every gift of Bhagavān with ceremony.

    Stories
    Dhruva's mantra-dīkṣā at five

    Five-year-old Dhruva, having walked alone into the forest, was found by Nārada, who gave him the dvādaśākṣara mantra and the procedure to worship Vāsudeva. The boy's tender turn toward the Lord was met immediately with Vedic provision — mantra, place, ritual (SB 4.8).

    The pattern is the same as Yaśodā's utthāna ceremony: when a soul makes a first movement Godward, the tradition surrounds that movement with Veda, brāhmaṇa, music, and offering.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.5

    ↗
    నన్దస్య పత్నీ కృతమజ్జనాదికం విప్రై: కృతస్వస్త్యయనం సుపూజితై: । అన్నాద్యవాస:స్రగభీష్టధేనుభి: సఞ్జాతనిద్రాక్షమశీశయచ్ఛనై: ॥ ౫ ॥
    nandasya patnī kṛta-majjanādikaṁ

    Important Words
    • vipraiḥ — by the brāhmaṇas
      Vipra — from vip, to tremble (with knowledge of brahman). A vipra is more than caste; he is one in whom Vedic knowledge has been internalized. Yaśodā's worship of vipras is the household acknowledging the chain of paramparā by which Kṛṣṇa's protection flows.
    • kṛta-svastyayanam — having engaged them in auspicious Vedic hymns
      Svasti (well-being) + ayana (going toward). The chanting itself is the going-toward-well-being. In Gauḍīya practice, this verse's principle becomes the maṅgalācaraṇa that opens every Vaiṣṇava function — the chant is the protection.
    • anna-ādya — by offering food grains and eatables
      Anna (food) + ādya (the eatables). Annadāna is the central act of household yajña. Prabhupāda emphasized: a Vaiṣṇava household feeds the brāhmaṇa first, then itself. The grain is not payment; it is the medium through which the household participates in the brāhmaṇa's tapas.
    • srak-abhīṣṭa-dhenubhiḥ — with flower garlands and desirable cows
      Srak (garland) + abhīṣṭa (desirable) + dhenu (milk-cow). Cow-charity is the highest dāna in Vedic culture, second only to kanyā-dāna. Vraja is go-loka — the cow-centered economy is not incidental to Kṛṣṇa's līlā; it is its substrate.
    After completing the bathing ceremony for the child, mother Yaśodā received the brāhmaṇas by worshiping them with proper respect and giving them ample food grains and other eatables, clothing, desirable cows, and garlands. The brāhmaṇas properly chanted Vedic hymns to observe the auspicious ceremony, and when they finished and mother Yaśodā saw that the child felt sleepy, she lay down on the bed with the child until He was peacefully asleep.
    Purport

    An affectionate mother takes great care of her child and is always anxious to see that the child is not disturbed even for a moment. As long as the child wants to remain with the mother, the mother stays with the child, and the child feels very comfortable. Mother Yaśodā saw that her child felt sleepy, and to give Him all facilities for sleep, she lay down with the child, and when He was peaceful, she got up to attend to her other household affairs.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    After completing the bathing ceremony for the child, mother Yaśodā received the brāhmaṇas by worshiping them with proper respect and giving them ample food grains and other eatables, clothing, desirable cows, and garlands. The brāhmaṇas properly chanted Vedic hymns to observe the auspicious ceremony, and when they finished and mother Yaśodā saw that the child felt sleepy, she lay down on the bed with the child until He was peacefully asleep.
    Commentary

    The brahmanas, after being honored by gifts of rice and cloth etc. (supujitaih), performed the rites. Because he had sleepy eyes (sanjatanidraksam), Krsna was put to rest (asisayat). Holding him motionlessly in her lap for fear he would wake up, she lay down with him on a cot under a cart situated in the large yard. When she understood that Krsna was fast asleep, she got up very gently.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    It should be understood that she had the permission and assistance of Nanda, since he was broad-minded. Kṛṣṇa was rubbed dry by Yaśodā and the gopīs. As well, he was anointed with gorocana and dressed. This was included in the bathing ceremony. Auspicious activities like svasti-vacana were performed (svastyayanam). The brāhmaṇas were honored with food, utensils (annādya) (some versions have ājya (ghee) instead) as well as cloth, garlands of gems, and objects dear to the gopīs or the brāhmaṇas and cows. The phrase can also read abhīṣṭa-dhenubhiḥ (desirable cows). She then put him in bed since his eyes were becoming sleepy. She did this gently because he was very tender and she did not want to disturb his sleep.

      His Holiness Prahladananada Swami· 1997-06-13· English
    Personal Guidance
    Mother Yaśodā's tender care of baby Kṛṣṇa shows that real bhakti is expressed through attentive, embodied service — not abstract sentiment.
    Practical Applications
    • Identify one person in your care today (child, parent, guest) and serve them with the same patient attentiveness Yaśodā gave Kṛṣṇa — staying until the task is truly complete.
    • Before a meal or important task, honor a brāhmaṇa or sādhu (in person, by call, or by remembrance) with food, dakṣiṇā, or sincere praṇāma, treating it as a non-negotiable household duty.
    • Refuse to rush through acts of seva today — whether feeding, settling a child, or hosting a guest — and let the work itself become your sādhana.
    Analogy

    A mother nurses her sleeping child and then rises to feed the brāhmaṇas at her door — the same hands that comfort the baby also serve the Lord's servants. Bhakti is not pulled between household and altar; in Yaśodā's hands, both are one continuous sevā to Kṛṣṇa, who lies asleep at the center.

    Stories
    Ambarīṣa's twin commitments

    Mahārāja Ambarīṣa ruled an empire and observed the ekādaśī fast with equal completeness. He fed brāhmaṇas, worshiped the deity, served Vaiṣṇavas, and never let the throne and the altar compete (SB 9.4).

    When Durvāsā tried to disrupt his vrata, the Sudarśana cakra defended him — the same protection Yaśodā's brāhmaṇas invoke here for Kṛṣṇa. The household that serves Vaiṣṇavas is already inside the Lord's defensive circle.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.6

    ↗
    ఔత్థానికౌత్సుక్యమనా మనస్వినీ సమాగతాన్ పూజయతీ వ్రజౌకస: । నైవాశ‍ృణోద్ వై రుదితం సుతస్య సా రుదన్ స్తనార్థీ చరణావుదక్షిపత్ ॥ ౬ ॥
    autthānikautsukya-manā manasvinī
    [autthaanikautsukya-manaa manasvinee]
    samāgatān pūjayatī vrajaukasaḥ
    [samaagataan poojayatee vrajaukasah]
    naivāśṛṇod vai ruditaṁ sutasya sā
    [naivaashrinod vai ruditam sutasya saa]
    rudan stanārthī caraṇāv udakṣipat
    [rudan stanaarthee charanaav udakshipat]
    Word-for-word
    autthānika - autsukya - manāḥ — mother Yaśodā was very busy celebrating the utthāna ceremony of her child manasvinī — very liberal in distributing food, clothing, ornaments and cows, according to necessity samāgatān — to the assembled guests pūjayatī — just to satisfy them vraja - okasaḥ — to the inhabitants of Vraja na — not eva — certainly aśṛṇot — did hear vai — indeed ruditam — the crying sutasya — of her child sā — mother Yaśodā rudan — crying stana - arthī — Kṛṣṇa, who was hankering to drink His mother’s milk by sucking her breast caraṇau udakṣipat — out of anger, threw His two legs hither and thither .

    Kṛṣṇa, who was hankering to drink His mother’s milk by sucking her breast
    caraṇau udakṣipat
    out of anger, threw His two legs hither and thither .
    Important Words
    • autthānika-autsukya-manāḥ — mind absorbed in celebrating the utthāna ceremony
      Autsukya (eagerness) + manāḥ (mind). Even devotional duty can crowd out direct remembrance if attention drifts to the ceremony's mechanics. Yaśodā's lapse is gentle and Kṛṣṇa-arranged — the verse warns sādhakas without scolding the mother.
    • stana-arthī — hankering to drink His mother's milk
      Stana (breast) + arthin (one seeking). Kṛṣṇa, the supreme enjoyer (paramaṁ-bhoktā), allows Himself to appear as one in need of His devotee's offering. The reversal — God as artha-arthin — is the heart of Vraja-bhāva. He is the receiver who depends on the giver.
    • caraṇau udakṣipat — out of anger, threw His two legs upward
      Caraṇau (the two feet) + udakṣipat (threw upward). The same lotus feet that the four Kumāras and Vedic mantras worship are here kicked angrily in a tantrum. Yoga-māyā veils the aiśvarya so that vātsalya may unfold — the Gauḍīya principle that aiśvarya hides for the sake of mādhurya.
    The liberal mother Yaśodā, absorbed in celebrating the utthāna ceremony, was busy receiving guests, worshiping them with all respect and offering them clothing, cows, garlands and grains. Thus she could not hear the child crying for His mother. At that time, the child Kṛṣṇa, demanding to drink the milk of His mother’s breast, angrily threw His legs upward.
    Purport

    Kṛṣṇa had been placed underneath a household handcart, but this handcart was actually another form of the Śakaṭāsura, a demon who had come there to kill the child. Now, on the plea of demanding to suck His mother’s breast, Kṛṣṇa took this opportunity to kill the demon. Thus He kicked Śakaṭāsura just to expose him. Although Kṛṣṇa’s mother was engaged in receiving guests, Lord Kṛṣṇa wanted to draw her attention by killing the Śakaṭāsura, and therefore He kicked that cart-shaped demon. Such are the pastimes of Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa wanted to draw the attention of His mother, but while doing so He created a great havoc not understandable by ordinary persons. These narrations are wonderfully enjoyable, and those who are fortunate are struck with wonder upon hearing of these extraordinary activities of the Lord. Although the less intelligent regard them as mythological because a dull brain cannot understand them, they are real facts. These narrations are actually so enjoyable and enlightening that Mahārāja Parīkṣit and Śukadeva Gosvāmī took pleasure in them, and other liberated persons, following in their footsteps, become fully jubilant by hearing about the wonderful activities of the Lord.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The liberal mother Yaśodā, absorbed in celebrating the utthāna ceremony, was busy receiving guests, worshiping them with all respect and offering them clothing, cows, garlands and grains. Thus she could not hear the child crying for His mother. At that time, the child Kṛṣṇa, demanding to drink the milk of His mother's breast, angrily threw His legs upward.
    Commentary

    Because she was busy exchanging pleasantries and giving ornaments, garlands, candana, oil and sindhura to the village women who had assembled for the festival, Yasoda did not hear the child crying from hunger on waking (stanarthi). He kicked up his feet in anger, with the thought "You cannot hear me cry, so the sound of the cart breaking will catch your attention."

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The reason that Yaśodā did not hear Kṛṣṇa crying was her attention (manasvinī), care, skill and faith in worshipping the guests since she had a generous heart. She was anxious that all the actions were done properly at the special celebration of her son rising from bed. Thus she worshipped the assembled people of Vraja with sandalwood and ornaments. The children who came there were kept at Kṛṣṇa’s side. She did not hear him cry at all (eva). Vai indicated certainty, like an oath. Otherwise she would have come, giving up the remaining duties. Wanting milk he cried. Since she was not there and kicked his feet in the air. This aptly describes the actions of an infant. According to Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa, he did this to kill the demon within the cart. Let that be. Because, as the Lord, he has the natural power to resolve everything, he cried because he wanted his mother’s milk (and this accomplished his purpose of killing the demon). Keeping in mind Kṛṣṇa’s infant nature, Śukadeva has described him in this way. Kṛṣṇa was absorbed in his infant pastimes controlled by parental affection, since his nature is to be controlled by the particular type of love within his devotee. Lokaval līlā-kaivalyāc ca: as we see in the world, the Lord’s motive is sport. (Brahma-sūtra 2.1.33)

      His Grace Sarvabhauma Prabhu· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    Even in the bustle of duty, Kṛṣṇa demands the mother's attention — kick aside the śakaṭa-load of distractions and turn back to Him today.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice the moment in your day when hospitality, hosting, or 'good work' eclipses direct remembrance of Kṛṣṇa, and pause to chant His name before resuming.
    • Identify one śakaṭa-shaped obstacle parked over your bhakti — a habit, a device, a worry — and decisively kick it aside this evening.
    • Refuse to dismiss the Lord's pastimes as mythology; read this verse aloud with Prabhupāda's purport and let yourself be struck with wonder.
    Analogy

    A potter so absorbed in shaping the rim of one pot does not hear the wheel-baby crying for milk — but the wheel is His, and He kicks it to remind her. So Kṛṣṇa, ignored even in the festival held for Him, lifts His soft foot to bring His mother's attention back to the center of the celebration.

    Stories
    Mārkaṇḍeya distracted from the Lord

    Mārkaṇḍeya Ṛṣi, long-lived through his japa, was once given a vision of pralaya. Floating on the cosmic waters, distracted by terror, he had forgotten the little child resting on the banyan leaf — until the child's toe gleamed and drew his attention back (SB 12.9).

    Kṛṣṇa's small foot, whether kicking a cart in Vraja or shining on a banyan leaf in pralaya, is the same instrument: it pulls the absorbed mind back to its proper center, the personal Bhagavān.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.7

    ↗
    అధ:శయానస్య శిశోరనోఽల్పక- ప్రవాలమృద్వఙ్‍‍ఘ్రిహతం వ్యవర్తత । విధ్వస్తనానారసకుప్యభాజనం వ్యత్యస్తచక్రాక్షవిభిన్నకూబరమ్ ॥ ౭ ॥
    adhaḥ-śayānasya śiśor ano ’lpaka-

    Important Words
    • pravāla — tender like a new leaf
      Pravāla — a coral, or a fresh sprouting leaf. The simile is precise: Kṛṣṇa's foot is as soft as a coral-pink sprout, yet it dismantles iron-bound axles. The aiśvarya hides inside mādhurya — the softest organ does the strongest work.
    • mṛdu-aṅghri-hatam — struck by His soft delicate feet
      Mṛdu (tender) + aṅghri (foot) + hata (struck). Aṅghri is the same word used in liturgical worship — caraṇāravinda, aṅghri-padma. The cart was kicked by the very feet on which devotees place their heads.
    • anaḥ — the cart (śakaṭa)
      Anaḥ — Vedic for cart or wagon. Tradition identifies the cart as Śakaṭāsura, an asura inhabiting the vehicle. The Gauḍīya reading: any neutral object can be the seat of a demoniac force when Kaṁsa-tattva sets it against the Lord's devotees.
    • vibhinna — broken
      From vi-bhid, to split apart. The same verb used for the shattering of bondage in mokṣa-contexts. Here the cart's pole splits; the principle is the same — Kṛṣṇa's touch shatters whatever structure stands against bhakti.
    Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa was lying down underneath the handcart in one corner of the courtyard, and although His little legs were as soft as leaves, when He struck the cart with His legs, it turned over violently and collapsed. The wheels separated from the axle, the hubs and spokes fell apart, and the pole of the handcart broke. On the cart there were many little utensils made of various metals, and all of them scattered hither and thither.
    Purport

    Śrīla Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura has commented on this verse as follows. When Lord Kṛṣṇa was of a very tender age, His hands and legs resembled soft new leaves, yet simply by touching the handcart with His legs, He made the cart fall to pieces. It was quite possible for Him to act in this way and yet not exert Himself very much. The Lord in His Vāmana avatāra had to extend His foot to the greatest height to penetrate the covering of the universe, and when the Lord killed the gigantic demon Hiraṇyakaśipu, He had to assume the special bodily feature of Nṛsiṁhadeva. But in His Kṛṣṇa avatāra, the Lord did not need to exert such energy. Therefore, kṛṣṇas tu bhagavān svayam: Kṛṣṇa is the Supreme Personality of Godhead Himself. In other incarnations, the Lord had to exert some energy according to the time and circumstances, but in this form He exhibited unlimited potency. Thus the handcart collapsed, its joints broken, and all the metal pots and utensils scattered. The Vaiṣṇava-toṣaṇī remarks that although the handcart was higher than the child, the child could easily touch the wheel of the cart, and this was sufficient to send the demon down to the earth. The Lord simultaneously pushed the demon to the earth and superficially broke the handcart.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa was lying down underneath the handcart in one corner of the courtyard, and although His little legs were as soft as leaves, when He struck the cart with His legs, it turned over violently and collapsed. The wheels separated from the axle, the hubs and spokes fell apart, and the pole of the handcart broke. On the cart there were many little utensils made of various metals, and all of them scattered hither and thither.
    Commentary

    The cart was struck by the soft feet of Krsna as if with very little force. In breaking the cart he did not extend his foot high upward like Vamana in breaking the shell of the unverse, nor did his feet become hard like Narasimha’s body to tear apart the hard body of Hiranyakasipu. This rare display of power of Krsna which does not contradict the sweetness of his childhood pastimes shows the completeness of Krsna. The car turned upside down and the utensils of gold and silver were scattered everywhere. The wheels and axle were dislocated and the handle was broken. From the Brahmanda Purana it is understood that because of the entrance of the asura into the cart, the wheels sank in the earth and the cart’s height decreased. Thus Krsna’s short legs were able to touch the cart.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Kṛṣṇa just touched the cart with a gesture of kicking it, using one foot which was soft like a new plant and which was very small, as he lay in a small bed beneath the axel of a huge cart outside the house. Or hatam can mean “moved.” Thus his foot contacted the axel. It was possible for Kṛṣṇa to touch it with his short leg because even though the axel was high, the wheels sunk in the ground because of the weight of the demon, or simply because of the power of the Lord. The demon had entered the cart invisibly and was destroyed invisibly. (No one saw this happen.) Because of his show of powers out of necessity during his childhood pastimes, his special glory in all conditions is shown. When Viṣṇu or avatāras like Nṛsiṁha kill demons, they display great prowess. Rāma showed only human-like pastimes during his childhood. Kṛṣṇa however shows his powers in harmony with his sweet human-like pastimes. In this way the highest sweetness of the Lord is accomplished. Arjuna speaks in Viṣṇu-dharma: tālocchritāgraṁ guru-bhāra-sāram āyāma-vistāravad adya-jātaḥ pādāgra-vikṣepa-vibhinna-bhāṇḍaṁ cikṣepa ko ‘nyaḥ śakatām yatha tvam The new born child, so small, destroyed the cart which was as high as a tāla tree, very wide and very heavy, which was broken by the kick of your foot. Who else could do this like you? The cart was as tall as a tāla tree which is six hastas high. Brahmā says in the Second Canto: tokena jīva-haraṇaṁ yad ulūki-kāyās trai-māsikasya ca padā śakaṭo ’pavṛttaḥ yad riṅgatāntara-gatena divi-spṛśor vā unmūlanaṁ tv itarathārjunayor na bhāvyam He killed Pūtanā when he was just an infant; he overturned the cart with his three-month-old foot; simply by his crawling between the two Arjuna trees which touched the heavens, he uprooted them. Such activities are not possible for other forms of the Lord. SB 2.7.27 The meaning is this. As a child he killed Pūtanā who had an owl’s body. When he crawled between the Arjuna trees he uprooted them. This is not possible (na bhāvyam) without the manifestation of special qualities of the Lord as Kṛṣṇa. It should be understood that because the cart was so huge, Yaśodā places Kṛṣṇa beneath it to sleep.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    With a desire to kill the demon, Kṛṣṇa just touched the cart with a gesture of kicking it, using one foot which was soft like a new plant and which was very small, as he lay in a small bed beneath (adhaḥ) the axel of a huge cart outside the house. Or hatam can mean “moved.” Thus his foot contacted the axel. The cart turned over, though it was huge and inhabited by the demon. This was a remarkable childhood pastime. Because of his show of powers out of necessity during his childhood pastimes, his special glory in all conditions is shown. When Viṣṇu or avatāras like Nṛsiṁha (as adult forms) kill demons, they display great prowess. Rāma showed only human-like pastimes during his infancy (without killing). But at this time, Kṛṣṇa shows his powers when he is an infant while acting as a normal human with sweetness. In this way the highest sweetness of the Lord is accomplished. Brahmā says in the Second Canto: tokena jīva-haraṇaṁ yad ulūki-kāyās trai-māsikasya ca padā śakaṭo ’pavṛttaḥ yad riṅgatāntara-gatena divi-spṛśor vā unmūlanaṁ tv itarathārjunayor na bhāvyam He killed Pūtanā when he was just an infant; he overturned the cart with his three-month-old foot; simply by his crawling between the two Arjuna trees which touched the heavens, he uprooted them. Such activities are not possible for other forms of the Lord. SB 2.7.27 The meaning is this. As a child he killed Pūtanā who had an owl’s body. When he crawled between the Arjuna trees he uprooted them. This is not possible (na bhāvyam) without the manifestation of special qualities of the Lord as Kṛṣṇa. Even in the future it is not possible. bhūmeḥ suretara-varūtha-vimarditāyāḥ kleśa-vyayāya kalayā sita-kṛṣṇa-keśaḥ | jātaḥ kariṣyati janānupalakṣya-mārgaḥ karmāṇi cātma-mahimopanibandhanāni ||

    Kṛṣṇa, with skillfully bound up hair, whose method of attainment is unknown to men, having made his appearance to relieve the earth of suffering caused by the armies of demons, and to relieve the devotees of their pain of separation, will perform activities which reveal his powers as the Lord. SB 2.7.26 Along with Balarāma (kalayā) Kṛṣṇa, who has three braid of black hair bound up (sita-kṛṣṇa-keṣaḥ), appeared to relieve the earth, which was suffering from armies of demons. This indicates his beauty and his intention of killing the demons. Or he whose aṁṣa is Nārāyaṇa with black hair bound up appeared to relieve the earth of its burden. With his methods undetected by people he appears and performs actions which reveal his extraordinary powers as the lord.

      His Holiness Haladhar Swami· 2020-07-07· Oriya

      His Holiness Sri Krishna Chaitanya Swami· 2019-11-24· English
    Personal Guidance
    Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead Himself, dispatches the Śakaṭāsura demon with a touch of His tender foot — His protection requires no exertion.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today where you are straining to engineer outcomes Kṛṣṇa could resolve effortlessly, and offer that strain to Him at your altar or in japa.
    • Refuse the impulse to measure spiritual strength by visible effort — chant one round of the Hare Kṛṣṇa mahā-mantra with full attention rather than many rounds distractedly.
    • Speak to Kṛṣṇa as Bāla-Gopāla today — small, soft-footed, personally present — not as an abstract force, and ask Him to break whatever 'cart' of accumulated saṁskāras blocks your bhakti.
    Analogy

    A king's signet ring, casually brushed against a counterfeit seal, dissolves the forgery without effort — not because the ring strained, but because the seal held no real authority against it. Kṛṣṇa's pravāla-soft foot meets the Śakaṭāsura the same way: no exertion, only the touch of the true.

    Stories
    Pūtanā at the breast

    Just before this episode, the same baby had drained the life-poisoned breast of Pūtanā as easily as He now kicks the cart — no struggle, no exertion, no special posture (SB 10.6).

    The pattern across the bāla-līlās is consistent: every demon Kaṁsa sends is dispatched with whatever organ of Kṛṣṇa happens to be nearest — His mouth, His foot, His hand. Bhagavān's protection does not require Him to assemble Himself for the task.

    Gajendra's lifted trunk

    When the elephant-king Gajendra, gripped by the crocodile, lifted his trunk with a single lotus and cried 'O Ādi-puruṣa,' Viṣṇu descended without delay and cut the crocodile with His cakra (SB 8.2–3).

    The operating principle is the same as Vraja's: the devotee's helpless cry is met by the Lord's effortless arm. Whether kicking a cart or wielding the cakra, the exertion is on the demon's side; for Bhagavān, it is līlā.

    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.8

    kathaṁ svayaṁ vai śakaṭaṁ viparyagāt
    katham
    how
    svayam
    by itself
    vai
    indeed
    śakaṭam
    the handcart
    viparyagāt
    became so heavily damaged, dismantled .
    Important Words
    • adbhuta-darśana — by seeing the wonderful sight
      Adbhuta (wonderful) + darśana (sight). Adbhuta-rasa is the rasa of astonishment — the Bhāgavatam catalogs it as the rasa that precedes śraddhā. The vraja-vāsīs' bewilderment here is the first stage of the recognition that will mature into the realization of 10.8 (Kṛṣṇa as everything).
    • viparyagāt — became dismantled, overturned
      From vi-pary-i, to go contrary, to reverse. The cart did not merely fall; it inverted. The verb hints at the Lord's līlā-principle of reversing apparent causation — what looked like accident is, viparyaya-wise, the Lord's arrangement.
    • ākulāḥ — perturbed, agitated
      From ā-kul, to be in a heap. The vraja-vāsīs' confusion is the standard reaction of those for whom Kṛṣṇa's aiśvarya is hidden by yoga-māyā. The Gauḍīya frame reads this not as their failure but as His mercy — He preserves their vātsalya by concealing His Godhood.
    • svayam — by itself
      Svayam — by its own self. The cowherds' word betrays their bewilderment: they cannot see an agent, so they speak as if the cart acted of itself. The deepest theology is hidden in this very word — Kṛṣṇa is svayaṁ-bhagavān, the only true svayam, and only He moves what moves 'by itself.'
    When mother Yaśodā and the other ladies who had assembled for the utthāna festival, and all the men, headed by Nanda Mahārāja, saw the wonderful situation, they began to wonder how the handcart had collapsed by itself. They began to wander here and there, trying to find the cause, but were unable to do so.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    When mother Yaśodā and the other ladies who had assembled for the utthāna festival, and all the men, headed by Nanda Mahārāja, saw the wonderful situation, they began to wonder how the handcart had collapsed by itself. They began to wander here and there, trying to find the cause, but were unable to do so.
    Commentary

    All the women headed by Yasoda who had gathered together for the ceremony, and Nanda and others, seeing that astonishing event, became dismayed. "How could the cart fall apart on its own?" they asked.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Yaśodā and all the women and Nanda and others, assembled for the festival, seeing the upset cart, becoming perturbed on seeing this astonishing event and, worrying because of the calamity, said (verb should be added) “How did the cart turn over by itself?” Instead of karmaṇi, sometimes parvaṇi is seen. The word vai indicates surprise.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Seeing the cart in that condition, with Kṛṣṇa sleeping beneath it, not in the house, all the people who had gathered for the festival, or others as well, became agitated in their hearts (ākulāḥ), because of the sudden overturning of the car. They feared some danger. Or they were overcome with prema (ākulāḥ) on constantly seeing astonishing Kṛṣṇa, or his astonishing pastimes. Not determining the cause they said, “How did the cart turn over on its own, without being kicked by a bull?” Vai expresses astonishment.

    Personal Guidance
    When Kṛṣṇa's protection looks like coincidence, the worldly eye searches for causes — the devotee recognizes the Lord's quiet hand and bows.
    Practical Applications
    • Recall one 'lucky' rescue from your past today and re-read it as Kṛṣṇa's unseen intervention rather than chance.
    • When something inexplicably goes right, refuse the reflex to credit yourself or circumstance — speak His name in thanks instead.
    • Notice where you, like the vraja-vāsīs, are still 'wandering here and there' for material explanations, and pause to remember the Lord is the cause of all causes.
    Analogy

    A child's father slips into the room at night and adjusts the blanket; in the morning the child wakes and asks who moved it, looking everywhere but to the father who lives in the same house. So the vraja-vāsīs hunt for a cause of the cart's collapse — the only One they should ask is the small Cause-of-all-causes lying right there.

    Stories
    Vidura at the door

    When Vidura returned to Hastināpura after his pilgrimage, the Kauravas could not see what had ripened in him. He looked the same — a brother, a counsellor — yet the kingdom's deepest events now turned on his unseen counsel (SB 3.1).

    Kṛṣṇa's hand in the world is often Vidura-shaped: present in plain sight, attributed to other causes. The devotee learns to read the unseen author behind ordinary surfaces.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.9

    ↗
    ఊచురవ్యవసితమతీన్ గోపాన్గోపీశ్చ బాలకా: । రుదతానేన పాదేన క్షిప్తమేతన్న సంశయ: ॥ ౯ ॥
    ūcur avyavasita-matīn
    [oochur avyavasita-mateen]

    [kshiptam etan na samshayah]
    Word-for-word
    ūcuḥ — said avyavasita - matīn — who had lost all intelligence in the present situation gopān — to the cowherd men gopīḥ ca — and to the ladies bālakāḥ — the children rudatā anena — as soon as the child cried pādena — with one leg kṣiptam etat — this cart was dashed apart and immediately fell dismantled na saṁśayaḥ — there is no doubt about it .
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    Important Words
    • avyavasita-matīn — those who had lost all intelligence in the present situation
      A-vy-ava-sita (un-determined) + mati (intelligence). The same buddhi-yoga vocabulary as the Gītā — when the situation transcends ordinary causation, ordinary mati fails. The verse positions childlike receptivity above adult analysis.
    • rudatā anena — by Him while crying
      Anena (by Him) — even Kṛṣṇa's crying is creative. Vraja-līlā treats His every bodily act, including tears and tantrums, as līlā-pradarśana. The asura is dispatched not in spite of His crying but through it.
    • kṣiptam etat — this was kicked apart
      Kṣipta — thrown, hurled. The same root as the Gītā's saṅkalpa-prabhavān kāmāṁs tyaktvā sarvān (6.24). The cart's destruction is a single decisive throw — the model of how Kṛṣṇa removes obstacles when the soul cries for Him.
    • na saṁśayaḥ — there is no doubt about it
      Saṁśaya — doubt, the obstacle Kṛṣṇa repeatedly cuts (Gītā 4.42). The children speak the very phrase Kṛṣṇa Himself uses in the Gītā. Pure śraddhā speaks with na saṁśayaḥ; sophisticated reasoning speaks with viparyaya.
    The assembled cowherd men and ladies began to contemplate how this thing had happened. “Is it the work of some demon or evil planet?” they asked. At that time, the small children present asserted that the cart had been kicked apart by the baby Kṛṣṇa. As soon as the crying baby had kicked the cart’s wheel, the cart had collapsed. There was no doubt about it.
    Purport

    We have heard of people’s being haunted by ghosts. Having no gross material body, a ghost seeks shelter of a gross body to stay in and haunt. The Śakaṭāsura was a ghost who had taken shelter of the handcart and was looking for the opportunity to do mischief to Kṛṣṇa. When Kṛṣṇa kicked the cart with His small and very delicate legs, the ghost was immediately pushed down to the earth and his shelter dismantled, as already described. This was possible for Kṛṣṇa because He has full potency, as confirmed in the Brahma-saṁhitā (5.32) : aṅgāni yasya sakalendriya-vṛttimanti paśyanti pānti kalayanti ciraṁ jaganti ānanda-cinmaya-sad-ujjvala-vigrahasya govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi Kṛṣṇa’s body is sac-cid-ānanda-vigraha, or ānanda-cinmaya-rasa-vigraha. That is, any of the parts of His ānanda-cinmaya body can act for any other part. Such are the inconceivable potencies of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. The Supreme Lord does not need to acquire these potencies; He already has them. Thus Kṛṣṇa kicked His little legs, and His whole purpose was fulfilled. Also, when the handcart broke, an ordinary child could have been injured in many ways, but because Kṛṣṇa is the Supreme Personality of Godhead, He enjoyed the dismantling of the cart, and nothing injured Him. Everything done by Him is ānanda-cinmaya-rasa, full transcendental bliss. Thus Kṛṣṇa factually enjoyed. The nearby children saw that actually Kṛṣṇa had kicked the wheel of the cart and this was how the accident happened. By the arrangement of Yoga-māyā, all the gopīs and gopas thought that the accident had taken place because of some bad planet or some ghost, but in fact everything was done by Kṛṣṇa and enjoyed by Him. Those who enjoy the activities of Kṛṣṇa are also on the platform of ānanda-cinmaya-rasa; they are liberated from the material platform. When one develops the practice of hearing kṛṣṇa-kathā, he is certainly transcendental to material existence, as confirmed in Bhagavad-gītā ( sa guṇān samatītyaitān brahma-bhūyāya kalpate ). Unless one is on the spiritual platform, one cannot enjoy the transcendental activities of Kṛṣṇa; or in other words, whoever engages in hearing the transcendental activities of Kṛṣṇa is not on the material platform, but on the transcendental, spiritual platform.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The assembled cowherd men and ladies began to contemplate how this thing had happened. "Is it the work of some demon or evil planet?" they asked. At that time, the small children present asserted that the cart had been kicked apart by the baby Kṛṣṇa. As soon as the crying baby bad kicked the cart's wheel, the cart had collapsed. There was no doubt about it.
    Commentary

    The children who had witnessed the event explained it to the gopis who could not discern what had happened. "Is this the work of some demon or evil planets?" they asked.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Unsettled because of the surprising incident, they could not determine the cause of the cart crashing. Perhaps it was a demon or evil spirit. The children had been looking at Kṛṣṇa, attracted by his sweetness. That cart was turned over by the foot of Kṛṣṇa when he cried. Pointing out Kṛṣṇa using the word anena (by him), they indicate that they directly saw it at that time: there is no doubt about this; we definitely saw this.

    Personal Guidance
    Kṛṣṇa's tiny kick dismantles the Śakaṭāsura — what looks accidental in our day is His arrangement; hearing such kathā lifts us beyond māyā.
    Practical Applications
    • When something in your day suddenly 'breaks' — a plan, a possession, a position — refuse the demon-or-bad-luck story and look for Kṛṣṇa's hand in the dismantling.
    • Read or hear one passage of kṛṣṇa-kathā today aloud, however briefly, and notice the shift from material anxiety to ānanda-cinmaya-rasa.
    • Loosen your grip on one thing you have been clutching as 'mine' — a project, an outcome, an object — and offer it inwardly to the small feet of Kṛṣṇa.
    Analogy

    Small children at a festival often see the truth the elders miss — a kingfisher among the lamps, a thief among the guests. The bālakāḥ at the broken cart see what Kaṁsa's spies and Vraja's elders both miss: Kṛṣṇa's foot did this. Innocence sees Bhagavān before sophistication does.

    Stories
    Prahlāda among the schoolchildren

    When Hiraṇyakaśipu's tutors taught artha and kāma, the child Prahlāda stood up and taught Viṣṇu-bhakti to his classmates instead (SB 7.5–6). The asura court could not credit a five-year-old's theology; the children themselves heard it gladly.

    Kṛṣṇa's witness often arrives through bālakāḥ — Prahlāda, the gopa-children of Vraja, the children at the broken cart. The verse here records a moment when the small ones see clearly what the grown gopas cannot.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.10

    ↗
    న తే శ్రద్దధిరే గోపా బాలభాషితమిత్యుత । అప్రమేయం బలం తస్య బాలకస్య న తే విదు: ॥ ౧౦ ॥

    [na te shraddadhire gopaa]
    bāla-bhāṣitam ity uta
    [baala-bhaashitam ity uta]
    aprameyaṁ balaṁ tasya
    [aprameyam balam tasya]

    [baalakasya na te viduh]

    na — not te — the cowherd men and ladies śraddadhire — put their faith (in such statements) gopāḥ — the cowherd men and women bāla - bhāṣitam — childish talk from the assembled children iti uta — thus spoken aprameyam — unlimited, inconceivable balam — the power tasya bālakasya — of the small baby Kṛṣṇa na — not te — the gopīs and gopas viduḥ — were aware of .

    Important Words
    • aprameyam — unlimited, inconceivable
      A-prameya — that which cannot be measured (pramā). Same root as pramāṇa (evidence). Kṛṣṇa's power is literally pramāṇa-atīta — beyond evidence, beyond inference. The Gauḍīya frame insists this is acintya, not unreal.
    • balam — the power
      Bala — strength. Distinguished in Vaiṣṇava theology from śakti (energy): bala is Kṛṣṇa's own potency, śakti is His energy expressed. The verse names Kṛṣṇa's own bala — what is being dismissed is the Lord's intrinsic power, not a borrowed faculty.
    • śraddadhire — put their faith
      Śrad-dhā — to place the heart. Śraddhā is the entry-condition of bhakti (Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu 1.4.15). The verse pinpoints exactly what is missing: not information (they have witnesses), but the heart-placing willingness to credit them.
    • bāla-bhāṣitam — childish talk
      Bāla (child) + bhāṣita (spoken). The dismissal-formula. Prabhupāda often warned that the world treats Vaiṣṇava preaching the same way — as children's talk — and that the preacher's job is to speak anyway, leaving the result to Kṛṣṇa.
    The assembled gopīs and gopas, unaware that Kṛṣṇa is always unlimited, could not believe that baby Kṛṣṇa had such inconceivable power. They could not believe the statements of the children, and therefore they neglected these statements as being childish talk.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The assembled gopīs and gopas, unaware that Kṛṣṇa is always unlimited, could not believe that baby Kṛṣṇa had such inconceivable power. They could not believe the statements of the children, and therefore they neglected these statements as being childish talk.
    Commentary

    Hearing the statements of the children, the cowherd men could not believe them.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    They all had complete knowledge since they were dear to the Supreme Lord. However, they did not perceive Kṛṣṇa’ unlimited powers (aprameyam) because the Supreme Lord cannot be measured, and particularly in this case, he was revealing his childhood pastimes (and thus hid his powers). Thus they did not believe the words of the children. The word te is mentioned twice to indicate another cause: they were overcome with the bliss of prema by which they took Kṛṣṇa as their son. That prema covers everything else. Similarly Balarāma is described being overcome with prema: śrutvaitad bhagavān rāmo vipakṣīya nṛpodyamam kṛṣṇaṁ caikaṁ gataṁ hartuṁ kanyāṁ kalaha-śaṅkitaḥ balena mahatā sārdhaṁ bhrātṛ-sneha-pariplutaḥ tvaritaḥ kuṇḍinaṁ prāgād gajāśva-ratha-pattibhiḥ Balarāma heard about these preparations of the inimical kings and how Kṛṣṇa had set off alone to steal the bride, he feared that a fight would ensue. Immersed in affection for his brother, he hurried to Kuṇḍina with a mighty army consisting of infantry and of soldiers riding on elephants, horses and chariots. SB 10.53.20-21 Śukadeva praises Yaśodā’s prema: nemaṁ viriṣco na bhavo na śrīr apy aṅga-saṁśrayā prasādaṁ lebhire gopī yat tat prāpa vimuktidāt Neither Brahmā, nor Śiva, nor even the goddess of fortune, who is always the better half of the Supreme Lord, can obtain from the Supreme Lord, the deliverer from this material world, such mercy as received by mother Yaśodā. SB 10.9.20 “They could not understand since they were overwhelmed by prema, but others’ statements could enlighten them.” But even cowherds did not believe the words, since children spoke them. If even (uta) the cowherds did not believe, definitely the gopīs could not believe them.

      His Holiness Prahladananada Swami· 2023-09-20· Slovenian
    Personal Guidance
    When Kṛṣṇa's aprameya power shows up in plain sight, the worldly mind dismisses it as bāla-bhāṣita — train yourself to take the small signs seriously.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today where you are dismissing a child's, a junior's, or a devotee's witness of Kṛṣṇa as 'just talk' — pause, listen again, and let it land.
    • Refuse the gopas' mistake: when a small mercy or unlikely rescue arrives in your day, name it as Kṛṣṇa's aprameya hand instead of crediting coincidence.
    • Speak one concrete glory of Kṛṣṇa to someone in your household today, even if you fear they will treat it as childish — your job is to say it, not to be believed.
    Analogy

    A diamond left on a child's palm looks like a glass bead to the visitors — they pat the child's head and reach for the bead, while the true gem stays exactly where it was placed. The vraja elders dismiss the children's testimony the same way: the witness is correct, but their categories cannot contain it.

    Stories
    Nārada at Dhruva's tapas

    When five-year-old Dhruva walked into the forest to meditate, the worldly were sure it would not last — a child's resolution. Nārada himself tried to dissuade him. But the boy's intelligence carried a strength the adult world could not measure, and he won the Lord's darśana (SB 4.8).

    The pattern of 10.10 repeats across the Bhāgavatam: aprameya power housed in a small form, dismissed by adults, recognized only later. The Vaiṣṇava trains to listen even when the speaker looks too small to be heard.

    sūktaiḥ
    by Vedic hymns
    stanam
    her breast
    apāyayat
    made the child suck .
    Important Words
    • graha-śaṅkitā — fearing some bad planet
      Graha (planet, but also 'seizer') + śaṅkita (suspecting). The grahas in Vedic astrology are subtle forces that seize the jīva's situation. Yaśodā's instinct to counter graha with mantra reflects the Vedic principle that subtle disturbances need subtle counter-measures, not gross ones.
    • kṛta-svastyayanam — performed an auspicious ritualistic ceremony
      Same compound as verse 5 — svasti + ayana, the going-toward-well-being. Repeated because the ceremony is the answer twice: at festival (utthāna) and at crisis (graha-bādha). Vaiṣṇava life moves through both registers with the same instrument.
    • sūktaiḥ — with Vedic hymns
      Sūkta — 'well-spoken,' a Vedic hymn. The plural is significant: Yaśodā does not rely on a single charm but commissions repeated, layered protection. The Gauḍīya parallel: chanting the mahā-mantra round after round, not as repetition but as layered shelter.
    • stanam apāyayat — made the child suck her breast
      Stana (breast) + apāyayat (caused to drink). The first response is not the mantra but the breast — Yaśodā restores Kṛṣṇa's bodily comfort before ritual. In Gauḍīya household-bhakti, care of the deity's body and the vipra's stomach precedes abstract worship.
    Thinking that some bad planet had attacked Kṛṣṇa, mother Yaśodā picked up the crying child and allowed Him to suck her breast. Then she called for experienced brāhmaṇas to chant Vedic hymns and perform an auspicious ritualistic ceremony.
    Purport

    Whenever there is some danger or some inauspicious occurrence, it is the custom of Vedic civilization to have qualified brāhmaṇas immediately chant Vedic hymns to counteract it. Mother Yaśodā did this properly and allowed the baby to suck her breast.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Thinking that some bad planet had attacked Kṛṣṇa, mother Yaśodā picked up the crying child and allowed Him to suck her breast. Then she called for experienced brāhmaṇas to chant Vedic hymns and perform an auspicious ritualistic ceremony.
    Commentary

    Yasoda had the brahmanas perform svastyana, chanting mantras to ward off raksasas.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This verse describes the actions of Yaśodā caused by her great affection. She feared spirits (graha) which steal children. She had auspicious rites performed with chanting of Vedic verses by brāhmaṇas for destroying Rākṣasas. After becoming peaceful, she fed her child breast milk.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This verse describes the actions of Yaśodā caused by her great affection. He cried so that she would not recognize him as the Lord. She feared spirits (graha) which steal children, or the influence of planets like Saturn. Or worried by his crying (āgraha-saṅkitāḥ) she picked him up. She had auspicious rites performed with chanting of Vedic verses of blessings for safety (sūktaiḥ) by brāhmaṇas, for destroying Rākṣasas. Then, relieved, she fed him.

      His Holiness Haladhar Swami· 2020-07-11· Oriya
    Personal Guidance
    When danger threatens, take shelter in Kṛṣṇa as Yaśodā took shelter in vipra-mantras — practical surrender, not panic, is the Vaiṣṇava response.
    Practical Applications
    • When alarm strikes today, pause and chant Kṛṣṇa's names before reacting — let the holy sound be your first counter-measure, not your last.
    • Identify one inauspicious situation you have been worrying over and request a qualified devotee or brāhmaṇa to chant or offer prayers on its behalf.
    • Refuse the modern reflex of treating crises as purely material problems — meet them with sādhana (japa, kīrtana, prayer) before logistics.
    Analogy

    A frightened mother who suspects an unseen enemy at the gate sends not for the soldiers but for the priests — she knows the real protection runs through mantra, not muscle. Yaśodā's first reflex when fear strikes is to nurse Kṛṣṇa and call the brāhmaṇas; the Vaiṣṇava household meets calamity with the breast and the Veda.

    Stories
    Kuntī's prayer in calamity

    When the Pāṇḍavas escaped Duryodhana's poisons, lac-house, and battles, Kuntī did not credit luck. She prayed to Kṛṣṇa, vipadaḥ santu naḥ śaśvat — 'let calamities come again and again, so that we may remember You' (SB 1.8.25).

    Yaśodā's response in 10.7.11 is the same Vaiṣṇava reflex in a less philosophical key: when fear strikes, do not negotiate with fear — turn to the Lord through His agents.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.7.12

    ↗
    పూర్వవత్ స్థాపితం గోపైర్బలిభి: సపరిచ్ఛదమ్ । విప్రా హుత్వార్చయాంచక్రుర్దధ్యక్షతకుశామ్బుభి: ॥ ౧౨ ॥

    Important Words
    • balibhiḥ — by the strong stout cowherd men
      Bali — strong. The same word as bali-rāja (Mahārāja Bali) and bali (offering). Strength offered in Kṛṣṇa's service is itself a kind of yajña. Vraja's gopas embody this: physical power placed at the Lord's feet, not stockpiled for the self.
    • hutvā — after performing a fire ceremony
      From hu — to offer into fire. Same root as huta (oblation) and havis (offering). The yajña-frame organizes the response to crisis: nothing is repaired in Vraja without first being offered. Action that bypasses Agni and the Lord is mere mending.
    • dadhi — curd
      The simplest Vraja substance — curd from the family cow. The verse lists dadhi, akṣata, kuśa, ambu — four humblest offerings. Vaiṣṇava worship insists that the simplest available pure substance, offered with bhakti, satisfies Kṛṣṇa fully (Gītā 9.26: patraṁ puṣpaṁ phalaṁ toyam).
    • akṣata — unbroken grains of rice
      A-kṣata — that which is not broken, not damaged. Unbroken rice symbolizes the integrity of the household after disturbance: the cart was broken; the offering is whole. The substance speaks the prayer.
    After the strong, stout cowherd men assembled the pots and paraphernalia on the handcart and set it up as before, the brāhmaṇas performed a ritualistic ceremony with a fire sacrifice to appease the bad planet, and then, with rice grains, kuśa, water and curd, they worshiped the Supreme Lord.
    Purport

    The handcart was loaded with heavy utensils and other paraphernalia. To set the cart back in its original position required much strength, but this was easily done by the cowherd men. Then, according to the system of the gopa-jāti, various Vedic ceremonies were performed to appease the calamitous situation.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    After the strong, stout cowherd men assembled the pots and paraphernalia on the handcart and set it up as before, the brāhmaṇas performed a ritualistic ceremony with a fire sacrifice to appease the bad planet, and then, with rice grains, kuśa, water and curd, they worshiped the Supreme Lord.
    Commentary

    The cart was restored to its original form and position by strong cowherdmen. This indicates that the cart was very large. They worshipped the cart because the cart was a main utensil of the cowherd men, being the abode of Laksmi, and the storehouse of accumulated wealth.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This verse shows that the brāhmaṇas also were unaware of Kṛṣṇa’s powers. Since strong cowherds set the cart properly, it is understood that cart must have been large and heavy. This was indicated previously in the quotation from the Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa. Again Yaśodā placed Kṛṣṇa beneath the cart without fear. The brāhmaṇas performed a graha-homa for general protection using ghee, with Vedic mantras like bhūr bhuvaḥ svāhā in order to eliminate insecurity and then worshipped the cart using kuśa and sacred water along with rice mixed with yogurt, since the cart was the main shelter of the cowherds.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Adambhitva (Non-hypocrisy / Authenticity)Vairagya (Detachment / Dispassion)Nishkama Karma (Selfless Action)Daksya (Resourcefulness / Skill)Vinaya (Humility / Modesty)

    • Padaseva Pr SB 10-07-12 Oriya
      His Grace Padaseva Prabhu· Oriya
    • BCaitanyaS SB 10-07-12 - Kick out the Shakatasura Within - 2012-03-04 ISKCON Chowpatty
      His Holiness Bhakti Caitanya Swami· 2012-03-04· English
    • DMS SB 10-07-12 - In A Sane Civilisation There Is A System For Eve
      His Holiness Devamrita Swami· English
    Personal Guidance
    When calamity strikes, restore order with steady hands and offer the day's work to Kṛṣṇa — practical action and devotional ritual together protect what we love.
    Practical Applications
    • After a disturbance today — a spilled task, a broken plan — restore the situation with calm, vigorous effort instead of brooding over the upset.
    • Before resuming normal work after a setback, pause for even one minute of remembrance — offer dadhi-akṣata-style simplicity (a flower, a tulasī leaf, a name) to Kṛṣṇa.
    • Refuse the modern split between 'practical fixing' and 'spiritual response' — do both, in sequence, the way the gopas did.
    Analogy

    When a sudden gust topples the lamps at the maṅgala-ārati, the pūjārī first sets the wicks upright and lights them again — then offers tulasī and water with steady hands. Action and worship are one unbroken motion. The gopas restoring the cart, the brāhmaṇas chanting and offering — both halves of one continuous sevā to Kṛṣṇa.

    Stories
    Yudhiṣṭhira after the war

    Having won the kingdom at terrible cost, Yudhiṣṭhira did not retreat into grief. On Kṛṣṇa's and Bhīṣma's instruction, he restored order — the aśvamedha, the rebuilding, the dāna to brāhmaṇas — and then returned to remembering the Lord (SB 1.9, MBh Śānti-parva).

    The gopas' simple sequence — fix the cart, then perform yajña — is the same dharma in miniature: restore the situation with sober hands, then re-anchor it in the Lord through ritual.
